4
Rolling Contacts

In this section we relax the assumption on the rigid grasping points. In particular, we assume that the robotic agents are connected to the object in terms of
rolling contacts
. As discussed before, this more natural approach to cooperative manipulation allows for a wider class of objects to be manipulated, and allows for modular manipulation scenarios where robots can be swapped to adjust the grasp online.

Figure 24
:
A robotic agent in contact with a rigid object via a rolling contact.

4.1
System Model

We provide here the model of the coupled system, which is slightly different with respect to that of Section
3.1
to account for the rolling contact constraints.
As before, we consider
N\in\mathbb{R}
robotic agents grasping a rigid object in
3
D space, with generalized variables
q_{i},\dot{q}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{i}}
. We relax the assumption of fully actuated agents by requiring
n_{i}\geq 3
,
\forall i\in\mathcal{N}
. Each agent has a smooth, convex “fingertip” (i.e. passive end-effector) of high stiffness that is
in contact with an object via a smooth contact surface. In addition to the end-effector frames
\mathcal{E}_{i}
, we add the contact frames
\mathcal{C}_{i}
, located at
p_{\scriptscriptstyle C_{i}}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
, with respect to the inertial frame;
\mathcal{C}_{i}
are defined as Gauss frames
[
117
]
, where one of the axes is defined orthonormal to the contact plane. We further define
p_{\scriptscriptstyle C_{i}/E_{i}}\coloneqq p_{\scriptscriptstyle C_{i}}-p_{\scriptscriptstyle E_{i}}
,
\forall i\in\mathcal{N}
. A visual representation of
the contact geometry for the
i
th agent is shown in Fig.
24
. The dynamics of the
i
th agent is given by
[
117
]

B_{i}\ddot{q}_{i}+C_{q_{i}}\dot{q}_{i}+{g}_{q_{i}}=-J_{h_{i}}^{\top}{f}_{\scriptscriptstyle C_{i}}+{\tau}_{i}

(69)

with the dynamics terms as in (
1
),
{f}_{\scriptscriptstyle C_{i}}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
is the contact force in
three dimensions
,
and
J_{h_{i}}\coloneqq J_{h_{i}}(q_{i},p_{\scriptscriptstyle C_{i}/E_{i}}):\mathbb{R}^{n_{i}+3}\to\mathbb{R}^{3\times n_{i}}
is the Jacobian matrix to the contact point, defined by

J_{h_{i}}({q}_{i},{p}_{\scriptscriptstyle C_{i}/E_{i}}){\coloneqq}\begin{bmatrix}I_{3}&-S({p}_{\scriptscriptstyle C_{i}/E_{i}})\end{bmatrix}J_{i}({q}_{i}),

where
J_{i}
is the manipulator Jacobian that maps
\dot{q}_{i}\mapsto{v}_{i}
, defined in the previous section. Note the difference of (
3
) and (
69
) due to the presence of the rolling contacts. Note also that disturbances are not taken into account here for simplicity.

The full hand Jacobian matrix is
J_{h}\coloneqq J_{h}(q,p_{\scriptscriptstyle EC})\coloneqq\text{diag}\{[J_{h_{i}}]_{i\in{N}}\}\in\mathbb{R}^{3N\times n}
, with
p_{\scriptscriptstyle EC}\coloneqq[p_{\scriptscriptstyle C_{1}/E_{1}}^{\top},\dots,p_{\scriptscriptstyle C_{N}/E_{N}}^{\top}]^{\top}

\in\mathbb{R}^{3N}
.
As before, we consider that the dynamical parameters (masses, moments of inertia) appearing in the terms
B_{i}
,
C_{q_{i}}
,
{g}_{q_{i}}
are
unknown
,
\forall i\in\mathcal{N}
. The dynamics (
69
) can be written in vector form as

B\ddot{q}+C_{q}\dot{q}+{g}_{q}=-J_{h}^{\top}f_{\scriptscriptstyle C}+\tau,

(70)

where
B\coloneqq\text{diag}\{[B_{i}]_{i\in\mathcal{N}}\}
,
C_{q}\coloneqq\text{diag}\{[C_{q_{i}}]_{i\in\mathcal{N}}\}

\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
,
{g}_{q}\coloneqq[{g}_{q_{1}}^{\top},\dots
,
{g}_{q_{N}}^{\top}]

⊤

\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
{f}_{\scriptscriptstyle C}\coloneqq[{f}_{\scriptscriptstyle C_{1}}^{\top},\dots,{f}_{\scriptscriptstyle C_{N}}^{\top}]^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{3N}
. With a slight abuse of notation, we assume that the set
\mathsf{S}
(
\mathsf{S}_{i}
) contains the configurations
q
(
q_{i}
) that yield a non-singular
J_{h}

(J_{h_{i}})
(in contrast to just
J
(
J_{i}
) of the case of rigid contacts)

A common assumption in the majority of the related literature is that the object center of mass is accurately known, which is typically not the case in practice. We assume here tracking of a traceable point
p_{o}
on the object surface instead of the center of mass
p_{\scriptscriptstyle O}
, whose information is considered unknown. Note that appropriate sensor equipment, e.g., cameras and markers, can accurately track such points in practice.
Hence, to remove the dependency on an unknown object center of mass, we perform a standard rigid body transformation to the conventional object dynamics as follows. Let
{R}_{o}\coloneqq{R}_{o}({\eta}_{\scriptscriptstyle o}):\mathbb{T}\to\mathbb{SO}(3)
be the respective rotation matrix of a frame attached to
{p}_{o}
, and
{x}_{o}:=[{p}_{o}^{\top},{\eta}_{o}^{\top}]^{\top}\in\mathbb{M}
,
{v}_{o}:=[\dot{{p}}_{o}^{\top},{\omega}_{o}^{\top}]^{\top}{\in\mathbb{R}^{6}}
denote the pose and generalized velocity of the object frame, with (without loss of generality)
{\eta}_{o}=\eta_{\scriptscriptstyle O}
,
R_{o}={R}_{\scriptscriptstyle O}
, and
{\omega}_{o}=\omega_{\scriptscriptstyle O}
. The position vector from
{p}_{o}
to the respective contact point is
{p}_{\scriptscriptstyle C_{i}/o}\coloneqq p_{\scriptscriptstyle C_{i}}-p_{o}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
,
\forall i\in\mathcal{N}
.
Moreover, define
p_{\scriptscriptstyle C_{i}/O}\coloneqq p_{\scriptscriptstyle C_{i}}-p_{\scriptscriptstyle O}
,
\forall i\in\mathcal{N}
, and
p_{\scriptscriptstyle OC}\coloneqq[p_{\scriptscriptstyle C_{1}/O}^{\top},\dots,p^{\top}_{\scriptscriptstyle C_{N}/O}]^{\top}
,
p_{\scriptscriptstyle oC}\coloneqq[p^{\top}_{\scriptscriptstyle C_{1}/o},\dots,p^{\top}_{\scriptscriptstyle C_{N}/o}]^{\top}
.

Given the rolling contacts, the conventional object dynamics with respect to the object center of mass are given by the Newton-Euler formulation:

M_{\scriptscriptstyle O}\dot{v}_{\scriptscriptstyle O}+{C}_{\scriptscriptstyle O}v_{\scriptscriptstyle O}+{g}_{\scriptscriptstyle O}=\bar{G}_{\mathcal{R}}f_{\scriptscriptstyle C}

(71)

with the dynamic terms as in (
5
), and

\bar{G}_{\mathcal{R}}:\mathbb{R}^{3N}\to\mathbb{R}^{6\times 3N}
is the grasp map,
defined by
\bar{G}_{\mathcal{R}}\coloneqq\bar{G}_{\mathcal{R}}(p_{\scriptscriptstyle OC})\coloneqq[\bar{G}_{\mathcal{R}_{1}},...,\bar{G}_{\mathcal{R}_{N}}]
where
\bar{G}_{\mathcal{R}_{i}}\coloneqq\bar{G}_{\mathcal{R}_{i}}(p_{\scriptscriptstyle C_{i}/O}):\mathbb{R}^{3}\to\mathbb{R}^{6\times 3}
, with

\bar{G}_{\mathcal{R}_{i}}(p_{\scriptscriptstyle C_{i}/O})\coloneqq\left[\begin{array}[]{c}I_{3}\\
S({p}_{\scriptscriptstyle C_{i}/O})\end{array}\right].

Note the difference of
\bar{G}_{\mathcal{R}}
with respect to the rigid contact-case (
10
).
We now perform a transformation of the aforementioned dynamics to account for
{p}_{o}
.
Let
J_{a}\coloneqq J_{a}(\eta_{\scriptscriptstyle O}):\mathbb{T}\to\mathbb{R}^{6\times 6}
be defined as:

{J_{a}({\eta}_{\scriptscriptstyle O})\coloneqq}\begin{bmatrix}I_{3}&S({R}_{\scriptscriptstyle O}{p}_{\scriptscriptstyle Oo}^{\mathcal{O}})\\
{0}&I_{3}\end{bmatrix}

(72)

where
{p}_{\scriptscriptstyle Oo}^{\mathcal{O}}\coloneqq R_{\scriptscriptstyle O}^{\top}(p_{\scriptscriptstyle O}-{p}_{o})
, such that
{v}_{\scriptscriptstyle o}=J_{a}{v}_{\scriptscriptstyle O}
.
Note that
{p}_{\scriptscriptstyle Oo}^{\mathcal{O}}
is constant.

Substitution of
{v}_{o}=J_{a}{v}_{\scriptscriptstyle O}
and left multiplication by
J_{a}^{\top}
in (
71
) yields the adjusted object dynamics with respect to
{p}_{o}
:

{M}_{o}{\dot{v}}_{o}+{C}_{o}{v}_{o}+{g}_{o}={G}_{\mathcal{R}}{f}_{\scriptscriptstyle C},

(73)

where

\displaystyle{M}_{o}\coloneqq

\displaystyle{M}_{o}(\eta_{\scriptscriptstyle O})\coloneqq J_{a}^{\top}M_{\scriptscriptstyle O}J_{a},

\displaystyle{C}_{o}\coloneqq

\displaystyle{C}_{o}(\eta_{\scriptscriptstyle O},\omega_{\scriptscriptstyle O})\coloneqq J_{a}^{\top}(M_{\scriptscriptstyle O}\dot{J}_{a}+C_{\scriptscriptstyle O}J_{a}),

\displaystyle{g}_{o}\coloneqq

\displaystyle{g}_{o}(\eta_{\scriptscriptstyle O})\coloneqq J_{a}^{\top}g_{\scriptscriptstyle O},

\displaystyle{G}_{\mathcal{R}}\coloneqq

\displaystyle{G}_{\mathcal{R}}(p_{\scriptscriptstyle oC})\coloneqq[{G}_{\mathcal{R}_{1}},\dots,{G}_{\mathcal{R}_{N}}]\coloneqq J_{a}^{\top}\bar{G}_{\mathcal{R}},

with
{G}_{\mathcal{R}_{i}}\coloneqq{G}_{\mathcal{R}_{i}}(p_{\scriptscriptstyle C_{i}/o})
, and

\displaystyle{G}_{\mathcal{R}_{i}}(p_{\scriptscriptstyle C_{i}/o})\coloneqq J_{a}^{\top}\bar{G}_{\mathcal{R}_{i}}=

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}I_{3}\\
-S(R_{\scriptscriptstyle O}{p}^{\mathcal{O}}_{\scriptscriptstyle Oo}))+S({p}_{\scriptscriptstyle C_{i}/O})\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}I_{3}\\
S(p_{\scriptscriptstyle C_{i}/o})\end{bmatrix},

Note that
\bar{G}_{\mathcal{R}}
does not depend on
{p}_{\scriptscriptstyle O}
. Note also by the relation
{p}_{\scriptscriptstyle O}={p}_{o}-{R}_{\scriptscriptstyle O}{p}^{\mathcal{O}}_{\scriptscriptstyle Oo}
, that
{M}_{o}
,
{C}_{o}
,
{g}_{o}
are functions of
{\eta}_{o}=\eta_{\scriptscriptstyle O}
,
{\omega}_{o}={\omega}_{\scriptscriptstyle O}
with dependency on the constant but unknown term
{p}^{\mathcal{O}}_{\scriptscriptstyle Oo}
. We also note the following relation that will be needed subsequently:

\displaystyle\bar{G}_{\mathcal{R}_{i}}^{\top}{v}_{\scriptscriptstyle O}=

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}I_{3}\\
S({p}_{\scriptscriptstyle C_{i}}-p_{\scriptscriptstyle O})\end{bmatrix}^{\top}\begin{bmatrix}I_{3}&S(R_{\scriptscriptstyle O}{p}_{\scriptscriptstyle Oo}^{\mathcal{O}})\\
0&I_{3}\end{bmatrix}{v}_{o}={G}_{\mathcal{R}_{i}}^{\top}{v}_{o}

(74)

Similarly to the agents, the object dynamic parameters appearing in the terms
{M}_{o}
,
{C}_{o}
,
{g}_{o}
are considered to be
unknown
.

The more practical consideration of rolling contacts, as opposed to a rigid grasp, requires no slip to occur between the agents and object by ensuring that each contact force remains inside the friction cone defined by:

\mathcal{F}_{\scriptscriptstyle C_{i}}(\mu_{f})\coloneqq\{{f}^{\scriptscriptstyle C_{i}}_{\scriptscriptstyle C_{i}}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:f_{\scriptscriptstyle C,n_{i}}\mu_{f}\geq\sqrt{f_{\scriptscriptstyle C,x_{i}}^{2}+f_{\scriptscriptstyle C,y_{i}}^{2}}\}

(75)

where
{f}^{\mathcal{C}_{i}}_{c_{i}}\coloneqq R_{\scriptscriptstyle C_{i}}^{\top}f_{\scriptscriptstyle C_{i}}=:(f_{\scriptscriptstyle C,x_{i}},f_{\scriptscriptstyle C,y_{i}},f_{\scriptscriptstyle C,n_{i}})
is the
i
th contact force written in frame
\mathcal{C}_{i}
, whose orientation is described by
R_{\scriptscriptstyle C_{i}}\coloneqq R_{\scriptscriptstyle C_{i}}(\eta_{\scriptscriptstyle C_{i}}):\mathbb{T}\to\mathbb{SO}(3)
,
\forall i\in\mathcal{N}
, (
\eta_{\scriptscriptstyle C_{i}}\in\mathbb{T}
being the respective Euler-angle orientation), with tangential force components
f_{\scriptscriptstyle C,x_{i}},f_{\scriptscriptstyle C,y_{i}}\in\mathbb{R}
and normal force component
f_{\scriptscriptstyle C,n_{i}}\in\mathbb{R}
,
\mu_{f}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
is the friction coefficient. The full friction cone is the Cartesian product of all the friction cones:
\mathcal{F}_{\scriptscriptstyle C}\coloneqq\mathcal{F}_{\scriptscriptstyle C_{1}}\times...\times\mathcal{F}_{\scriptscriptstyle C_{n}}
.

In practice, it is common to approximate the friction cone by an inscribed pyramid with
l_{f}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
sides. The set associated with this pyramid is defined as

\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\scriptscriptstyle C_{i}}(\mu_{f})\coloneqq\{f^{\scriptscriptstyle C_{i}}_{\scriptscriptstyle C_{i}}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:\Lambda_{i}(\mu_{f})f^{\scriptscriptstyle C_{i}}_{\scriptscriptstyle C_{i}}\succeq 0\},

(76)

where
\Lambda_{i}(\mu_{f})\in\mathbb{R}^{l_{f}\times 3}
. The overall friction pyramid is then
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\scriptscriptstyle C}(\mu_{f})\coloneqq\{f^{\scriptscriptstyle C}_{\scriptscriptstyle C}\in\mathbb{R}^{3N}:\Lambda(\mu_{f})f^{\scriptscriptstyle C}_{\scriptscriptstyle C}\succeq 0\}
, where
f^{\scriptscriptstyle C}_{\scriptscriptstyle C}\coloneqq[(f^{\scriptscriptstyle C_{1}}_{\scriptscriptstyle C_{1}})^{\top},\dots,(f^{\scriptscriptstyle C_{N}}_{\scriptscriptstyle C_{N}})^{\top}]^{\top}
, and
\Lambda\coloneqq\text{diag}\{[\Lambda_{i}]_{i\in\mathcal{N}}\}
.

When the contact points do not slip, the grasp relation
J_{h}\dot{q}=\bar{G}_{\mathcal{R}}^{\top}{v}_{\scriptscriptstyle O}
holds
[
98
]
, which, after substituting (
74
), becomes:

{v}_{\scriptscriptstyle C}\coloneqq J_{h}\dot{q}={G}_{\mathcal{R}}^{\top}{v}_{o},

(77)

where
{v}_{\scriptscriptstyle C}\coloneqq[{v}_{\scriptscriptstyle C_{1}}^{\top},\dots,{v}_{\scriptscriptstyle C_{N}}^{\top}]^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{3n}
is the vector of contact velocities.

As in Section
3.3
, we use for the object orientation the unit quaternion choice
{\zeta}_{\scriptscriptstyle O}\coloneqq[\varphi_{\scriptscriptstyle O},{\epsilon}_{\scriptscriptstyle O}^{\top}]^{\top}\in\mathbb{S}^{3}
.
Let hence now a desired pose trajectory,
p_{\textup{d}}:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
,
{\zeta}_{\textup{d}}\coloneqq[\varphi_{\textup{d}},\epsilon_{\textup{d}}^{\top}]^{\top}:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\to\mathbb{S}^{3}
, to be tracked by
{x}_{o}
.
To that end, similar to Section
3.3
, we define the position error
{e}_{p_{o}}\coloneqq{p}_{o}-{p}_{\textup{d}}
as well as the quaternion product
{e}_{\zeta}\coloneqq{\zeta}_{\textup{d}}\cdot{\zeta}_{\scriptscriptstyle O}^{+}
. The aim is then to regulate
{e}_{p_{o}}
to zero and
{e}_{\zeta}
to
[\pm 1,{0}^{\top}]^{\top}
. Moreover, we aim at ensuring that the agents are always in contact with the object and slipping is avoided.
Formally, the problem is defined as follows.

Problem 2.3
.

Given a desired bounded, smooth object pose trajectory defined by
{p}_{\textup{d}}:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
,
{\zeta}_{\textup{d}}:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\to\mathbb{S}^{3}
, with bounded first and second derivatives, as well as uncertain agent and object dynamic parameters involved in (
69
) and (
73
), respectively, determine a control law
\tau
in (
70
) such that the following conditions hold:

1.

\lim_{t\to\infty}\left({e}_{p_{o}}(t),{e}_{\zeta}(t)\right)=\left({0},[\pm 1,{0}^{\top}]^{\top}\right)

2.

{f}^{\scriptscriptstyle C_{i}}_{\scriptscriptstyle C_{i}}(t)\in\mathcal{F}_{\scriptscriptstyle C_{i}},\forall t>0
,
i\in\mathcal{N}
.

In order to solve the aforementioned problem the following assumptions are made for the grasp:

Assumption 2.10
.

The grasp consists of
N\geq 3
agents with non-collinear contact points and
\textup{Null}(\bar{G}_{\mathcal{R}})\bigcap{\textup{Int}(\mathcal{F}_{\scriptscriptstyle C})}\neq\emptyset
.

Assumption 2.11
.

The matrix
J_{h}(q)
is non-singular, and the contact points do not exceed the fingertip surface.

Remark 2.9
.

Note that
N\geq 3
agents with non-collinear contact points ensures
\bar{G}_{\mathcal{R}}
is full row rank
[
117
]
. The condition that
\textup{null}(\bar{G}_{\mathcal{R}})\bigcap{\textup{Int}(\mathcal{F}_{\scriptscriptstyle C})}\neq\emptyset
ensures the existence of a contact force that lies within the friction cone and yields a desired object wrench, which is called the force-closure condition
[
98
]
. Force-closure depends on the initial grasp, and can be ensured by existing high-level grasp planning methods
[
118
]
. Moreover, by incorporating optimization techniques, as e.g. in
[
119
]
, we can enforce prevention of excessive rolling of the contacts and thus relax the respective part of Assumption
2.11
. Finally, the non-singular condition of
J_{h}
intuitively implies that tracking the desired reference trajectory does not force the agents through such singular configurations (such an assumption was also considered in the case of rigid grasps).
This can also be achieved by exploiting internal motions of redundant agents (
n_{i}>3
).

We also assume that the contact vectors
R_{i}^{\top}{p}_{\scriptscriptstyle C_{i}/E_{i}}
and their derivatives are measured accurately online,
\forall i\in\mathcal{N}
. This can be achieved either by the use of appropriate tactile sensors or forward simulation of the contact dynamics
[
117
]
. By also assuming the geometry of the fingertips known, we can also compute
\eta_{\scriptscriptstyle C_{i}}
and hence
R_{\scriptscriptstyle C_{i}}
online,
\forall i\in\mathcal{N}
.
Finally, note that
B_{i}(\cdot)
are positive definite, and
\dot{B}_{i}(\cdot)-2C_{q_{i}}(\cdot)
are skew-symmetric,
\forall i\in\mathcal{N}
, similarly to
M_{i}(\cdot)
, and
\dot{M}_{i}(\cdot)-2C_{i}(\cdot)
.

In the following, we present two
adaptive
control schemes for the solution of Problem
2.3
, a centralized one, where one computer unit (or a “leader” agent) computes the input commands for the entire team, as well as a decentralized one, based on event-triggered inter-agent communication.

4.2
Centralized Scheme

This section presents the centralized proposed control scheme, which employs adaptive control techniques for the compensation of the dynamic uncertainties of the agent and object present in the problem setup.

Without loss of generality, we assume that
n_{i}=3
,
\forall i\in\mathcal{N}
, i.e., the agents are not redundant. The proposed solution can be trivially extended to redundant cases, e.g., by following the analysis of
[
117
, Chapter 6]
.
By combining the agent and object dynamics (
70
), (
73
) as well as (
77
), we can obtain the coupled dynamics

{\widetilde{B}}{\dot{v}}_{o}+\widetilde{C}_{q}{v}_{o}+\widetilde{g}_{q}={G}_{\mathcal{R}}J_{h}^{-T}\tau,

(78)

where

\displaystyle\widetilde{B}\coloneqq

\displaystyle\widetilde{B}(\check{x})\coloneqq{M}_{o}+{G}_{\mathcal{R}}J_{h}^{-T}BJ_{h}^{-1}{G}_{\mathcal{R}}^{\top},

\displaystyle\widetilde{C}_{q}\coloneqq

\displaystyle\widetilde{C}_{q}(\check{x},\dot{\check{x}})\coloneqq{C}_{o}+{G}_{\mathcal{R}}J_{h}^{-T}\big(C_{q}J_{h}^{-1}{G}_{\mathcal{R}}^{\top}+B\frac{d}{dt}(J_{h}^{-1}{G}_{\mathcal{R}}^{\top})\big),

\displaystyle\widetilde{g}_{q}\coloneqq

\displaystyle\widetilde{g}_{q}(\check{x})\coloneqq{g}_{o}+{G}_{\mathcal{R}}J_{h}^{-T}g_{q},

and
\check{x}\coloneqq[\eta_{\scriptscriptstyle O}^{\top},{q}^{\top},p_{\scriptscriptstyle EC}^{\top},{p}_{\scriptscriptstyle oC}^{\top}]^{\top}\in\mathbb{T}\times\mathsf{S}\times\mathbb{R}^{6N}
.
The following lemma states useful properties of (
78
):

Lemma 2.2
.

The matrix
\widetilde{B}
is symmetric and positive-definite, and the matrix
\dot{\widetilde{B}}-2\widetilde{C}_{q}
is skew-symmetric.

Proof.

The proof is similar to the one of Lemma
2.1
and is omitted.
∎

Next, we proceed to parameterizing the dynamics with respect to constant but unknown dynamic parameters, similarly to the case of rigid contacts.
In particular, the left-hand side of the object dynamics (with respect to
{p}_{o}
) is parameterized as:

{M}_{o}({\eta}_{o})\dot{{v}}_{o}+{C}_{o}({\eta}_{o},{\omega}_{o}){v}_{o}+g_{o}=Y_{\mathcal{R}_{o}}({\eta}_{o},{\omega}_{o},{v}_{o},\dot{{v}}_{o}){\vartheta}_{\mathcal{R}_{o}},

where
{\vartheta}_{\mathcal{R}_{o}}\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell_{\mathcal{R}_{o}}}
,
{\ell_{\mathcal{R}_{o}}}\in\mathbb{N}
, is a vector containing the unknown object dynamic parameters, similarly to
\vartheta_{\scriptscriptstyle O}
defined in Section
3.3
, but also including the term
{p}^{\mathcal{O}}_{\scriptscriptstyle Oo}
, introduced in (
72
), and

Y_{\mathcal{R}_{o}}:\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}^{15}\to\mathbb{R}^{6\times\ell_{\mathcal{R}_{o}}}
is the respective (known) regressor matrix.
Similarly, the part of (
78
) that concerns the robotic agents can be linearly parameterized as:

\displaystyle B_{i}J_{h_{i}}^{-1}{G}_{\mathcal{R}_{i}}^{\top}\dot{{v}}_{o}+\left(B_{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\left(J_{h_{i}}^{-1}{G}_{\mathcal{R}_{i}}^{\top}\right)+C_{q_{i}}J_{h_{i}}^{-1}{G}_{\mathcal{R}_{i}}^{\top}\right){v}_{o}

\displaystyle+g_{q_{i}}=

\displaystyle Y_{\mathcal{R}_{i}}(\check{x}_{i},\dot{\check{x}}_{i},{v}_{o},\dot{{v}}_{o})\vartheta_{\mathcal{R}_{i}},

with
\check{x}_{i}\coloneqq[\eta_{o}^{\top},q_{i}^{\top},p^{\top}_{\scriptscriptstyle C_{i}/E_{i}},p^{\top}_{\scriptscriptstyle C_{i}/o}]^{\top}\in\mathbb{T}\times\mathsf{S}_{i}\times\mathbb{R}^{6}
,
Y_{\mathcal{R}_{i}}:\mathbb{T}\times\mathsf{S}_{i}\times\mathbb{R}^{30}\to\mathbb{R}^{3\times\ell_{\mathcal{R}}}
being agent
i
’s regressor matrix, and
\vartheta_{\mathcal{R}_{i}}\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell_{\mathcal{R}}}
,
\ell_{\mathcal{R}}\in\mathbb{N}
the respective vector of unknown, constant parameters. The aforementioned parameterization is written in vector form:

\displaystyle BJ_{h}^{-1}{G}_{\mathcal{R}}^{\top}\dot{{v}}_{o}+\left(B\frac{\partial}{\partial t}(J_{h}^{-1}{G}_{\mathcal{R}}^{\top})+C_{q}J_{h}^{-1}G_{\mathcal{R}}^{\top}\right){v}_{o}+g_{q}=Y_{\mathcal{R}}(\check{x},\dot{\check{x}},{v}_{o},\dot{{v}}_{o}){\vartheta_{\mathcal{R}}},

where
Y_{\mathcal{R}}\coloneqq Y_{\mathcal{R}}(\check{x},\dot{\check{x}},{v}_{o},\dot{{v}}_{o})

\coloneqq

\textup{diag}\{[Y_{\mathcal{R}_{i}}]_{i\in\mathcal{N}}\}

\in

\mathbb{R}^{3N\times\ell_{\mathcal{R}}}
, and
\vartheta_{\mathcal{R}}

\coloneqq

[\vartheta_{\mathcal{R}_{1}}^{\top},\dots,\vartheta_{\mathcal{R}_{N}}^{\top}]^{\top}

\in

\mathbb{R}^{N\ell_{\mathcal{R}}}
.

Therefore, the left-hand side of the coupled dynamics (
78
) can be written as

\displaystyle\widetilde{B}\dot{{v}}_{o}+\widetilde{C}_{q}{v}_{o}+\widetilde{g}_{q}=Y_{\mathcal{R}_{o}}({\eta}_{o},{\omega}_{o},{v}_{o},\dot{{v}}_{o})\vartheta_{\mathcal{R}_{o}}+{G}_{\mathcal{R}}J_{h}^{-T}Y_{\mathcal{R}}(\check{{x}},\dot{\check{{x}}},{v}_{o},\dot{{v}}_{o}){\vartheta_{\mathcal{R}}}

(79)

Let now
\hat{\vartheta}_{\mathcal{R}}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\ell_{\mathcal{R}}}
,
\hat{\vartheta}_{\mathcal{R}_{o}}\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell_{\mathcal{R}_{o}}}
, be the estimates of
{\vartheta}_{\mathcal{R}}
and
\vartheta_{\mathcal{R}_{o}}
, respectively, by the agents, and the respective errors

e_{\mathcal{R}_{\vartheta}}\coloneqq\hat{\vartheta}_{\mathcal{R}}-\vartheta_{\mathcal{R}}
, and
{e}_{\mathcal{R}_{\vartheta},o}\coloneqq\hat{\vartheta}_{\mathcal{R}_{o}}-{\vartheta}_{\mathcal{R}_{o}}
.

We provide next the proposed control protocol.
First, we design the reference velocity signal
{v}_{f_{o}}\in\mathbb{R}^{6}
and the associated velocity error
{e}_{v_{o}}
as

\displaystyle{v}_{f_{o}}

\displaystyle:=v_{\textup{d}}-Ke_{\mathcal{R}}\coloneqq\begin{bmatrix}\dot{p}_{\text{d}}\\
{\omega}_{\textup{d}}\end{bmatrix}-\begin{bmatrix}{k}_{p}{e}_{p_{o}}\\
-{k}_{\eta}\displaystyle\frac{{e}_{\epsilon}}{e_{\varphi}^{3}}\end{bmatrix}

(80a)

\displaystyle{e}_{v_{o}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq{v}_{o}-{v}_{f_{o}},

(80b)

where
{K}=\text{diag}\{{k}_{p}I_{3},{k}_{\eta}I_{3}\}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
is the positive definite gain matrix used in (
21
),
e_{\mathcal{R}}\coloneqq[{e}^{\top}_{p_{o}},-\frac{{e}^{\top}_{\epsilon}}{e_{\varphi}^{3}}]^{\top}
, and
{v}_{\text{d}}\coloneqq v_{\text{d}}(t)\coloneqq[\dot{p}_{\text{d}}^{\top},{\omega}_{\text{d}}^{\top}]^{\top}
. Note the difference in the definition of
e_{\mathcal{R}}
and
e
from (
21
), which will account tot stabilizing the scalar quaternion error
e_{\varphi}
to either
1
or
-1
, depending on
e_{\varphi}(0)
, while guaranteeing that
e_{\varphi}(t)\neq 0
,
\forall t\geq 0
(provided that
e_{\varphi}(0)\neq 0
), and rendering thus (
80a
) well defined.

We design now the control protocol as
\tau:\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{R}}\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
, with

\displaystyle\tau\coloneqq\tau(\chi_{\mathcal{R}},t)=

\displaystyle Y_{r}{\hat{\vartheta}_{\mathcal{R}}}+J_{h}^{\top}({G_{\mathcal{R}}^{\dagger}}{f}_{\text{d}}+{f}_{\text{int}}),

(81)

where
\chi_{\mathcal{R}}\coloneqq[\check{x}^{\top},\dot{\check{x}}^{\top},e_{\mathcal{R}}^{\top},e_{v_{o}}^{\top},\hat{\vartheta}_{\mathcal{R}}^{\top},\hat{\vartheta}_{\mathcal{R}_{o}}^{\top},\eta_{\scriptscriptstyle C}^{\top}]^{\top}
,
\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{R}}\coloneqq\{\chi_{\mathcal{R}}\in\mathbb{T}^{N+1}\times\mathsf{S}\times\mathbb{R}^{15N+15+N\ell_{\mathcal{R}}+\ell_{\mathcal{R}_{o}}}:e_{\varphi}\neq 0\}
,
\eta_{\scriptscriptstyle C}\coloneqq[\eta_{\scriptscriptstyle C_{1}}^{\top},\dots,\eta_{\scriptscriptstyle C_{N}}^{\top}]^{\top}
,

G_{\mathcal{R}}^{\dagger}
is the Moore-Penrose pseudoinverse of
G_{\mathcal{R}}
,
{f}_{\text{d}}\coloneqq Y_{o_{r}}\hat{\vartheta}_{\mathcal{R}_{o}}-{e_{\mathcal{R}}}-K_{v}{e}_{v_{o}}
with
K_{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{6\times 6}
the positive definite gain matrix used in (
23
),
Y_{r}\coloneqq Y_{\mathcal{R}}(\check{{x}},\dot{\check{{x}}},{v}_{f_{o}},\dot{v}_{f_{o}})
,
Y_{o_{r}}\coloneqq Y_{\mathcal{R}_{o}}({\eta}_{o},{\omega}_{o},{v}_{f_{o}},\dot{v}_{f_{o}})
,
and
{f}_{\text{int}}\coloneqq f_{\text{int}}(q,\eta_{\scriptscriptstyle C}):\mathsf{S}\times\mathbb{T}^{N}\to\mathbb{R}^{3N}
is a term in the nullspace of
G_{\mathcal{R}}
to prevent contact slip, which will be designed later. Moreover, we design the adaptation signals

\displaystyle\dot{{\hat{\vartheta}}}_{\mathcal{R}}

\displaystyle={\text{Proj}({\hat{\vartheta}_{\mathcal{R}}},{-\Gamma}Y_{r}^{\top}J_{h}^{-1}G_{\mathcal{R}}^{\top}{e}_{v_{o}}),}

(82a)

\displaystyle\dot{{\hat{\vartheta}}}_{\mathcal{R}_{o}}

\displaystyle=\text{Proj}({\hat{\vartheta}_{\mathcal{R}_{o}}},{-\Gamma_{o}}Y_{o_{r}}^{\top}{e}_{v_{o}}),

(82b)

where
\Gamma\in\mathbb{R}^{N\ell_{\mathcal{R}}\times N\ell_{\mathcal{R}}},\Gamma_{o}\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell_{\mathcal{R}_{o}}\times\ell_{\mathcal{R}_{o}}}
are constant positive definite gain matrices (as in (
25
)), and
\text{Proj}()
is the projection operator, which satisfies

[
110
]
:

(\hat{y}-{y})^{\top}(W^{-1}\text{Proj}({y},W{z})-{z})\leq 0,

(83)

for any symmetric positive definite
W\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell_{z}\times\ell_{z}}
, and
\forall\hat{y},{y},{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell_{z}}
, for some
\ell_{z}\in\mathbb{N}
.
Moreover, by appropriately choosing the initial conditions of the estimates
{\hat{\vartheta}_{\mathcal{R}}}(0)
,
{\hat{\vartheta}_{\mathcal{R}_{o}}}(0)
, we guarantee via the projection operator that
{\hat{\vartheta}_{\mathcal{R}}}(t)
,
{\hat{\vartheta}_{\mathcal{R}_{o}}}(t)
will stay uniformly bounded in predefined sets defined by finite constants
\bar{\vartheta}_{\mathcal{R}}
,
\bar{\vartheta}_{\mathcal{R}_{o}}
, i.e.,

\|\hat{\vartheta}_{\mathcal{R}}(t)\|\leq\bar{{\hat{\vartheta}}}_{\mathcal{R}}
,
\|\hat{\vartheta}_{\mathcal{R}_{o}}(t)\|\leq\bar{\hat{\vartheta}}_{\mathcal{R}_{o}}
,
\forall t\geq 0
. Hence, we can achieve the boundedness of the respective errors as

\displaystyle\|{e}_{\vartheta_{\mathcal{R}}}(t)\|

\displaystyle\leq\bar{e}_{\vartheta_{\mathcal{R}}}\coloneqq\bar{\vartheta}_{\mathcal{R}}+\|\vartheta_{\mathcal{R}}\|

(84a)

\displaystyle\|{e}_{\vartheta_{\mathcal{R},o}}(t)\|

\displaystyle\leq\bar{e}_{\vartheta_{\mathcal{R},o}}\coloneqq\bar{\vartheta}_{\mathcal{R}_{o}}+\|\vartheta_{\mathcal{R}_{o}}\|.

(84b)

More details can be found in
[
110
, Chapter 11]
.

We design next the internal force component
{f}_{\text{int}}
to guarantee slip prevention.
Slip is addressed by ensuring the contact forces remain inside the friction cone as specified in (
75
). From (
76
), we have to guarantee that
\Lambda_{i}(\mu_{f})R_{\scriptscriptstyle C_{i}}^{\top}f_{\scriptscriptstyle C_{i}}\succeq 0,\forall i\in\mathcal{N}
, or in vector form,

\Lambda(\mu_{f})R_{\scriptscriptstyle C}^{\top}{f}_{\scriptscriptstyle C}\succeq 0,

(85)

where
R_{\scriptscriptstyle C}\coloneqq R_{\scriptscriptstyle C}(\eta_{\scriptscriptstyle C})\coloneqq\text{diag}\{[R_{\scriptscriptstyle C_{i}}]_{i\in\mathcal{N}}\}
.

The design of the internal force component,
{f}_{\text{int}}
, to ensure (
85
) is performed as follows. First,
{f}_{\text{int}}
must be in the nullspace of
G_{\mathcal{R}}
, i.e.,
G_{\mathcal{R}}{f}_{\text{int}}=0
. Second, the internal force must satisfy (
85
). Third, the normal component of the internal force with respect to the contact plane must always be positive (i.e. the manipulators cannot ‘‘pull” on the contact point). To enforce this condition we design
1
1

1

We use the notation
{f}_{\text{int}}=[{f}_{\text{int}_{1}}^{\top},\dots,{f}_{\text{int}_{N}}^{\top}]^{\top}
.

{f}_{\text{int}_{i}}=f^{\prime}_{\text{int}}R_{\scriptscriptstyle C_{i}}{\ell}_{\text{int},i}
, where
{\ell}_{\text{int},i}\coloneqq[\ell_{\text{int},i_{x}},\ell_{\text{int},i_{y}},\ell_{\text{int},i_{z}}]^{\top}
is the internal force direction in the contact frame
\mathcal{C}_{i}
,
i\in\mathcal{N}
, and
f^{\prime}_{\text{int}}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
is a gain parameter to be designed. Without loss of generality let
\ell_{\text{int},i_{z}}
be aligned with the normal direction of the contact frame such that
\ell_{\text{int},i_{z}}>0
,
i\in\mathcal{N}
, ensures that only pushing forces are applied at each contact. Satisfaction of the aforementioned conditions is done by solving the following convex quadratic program to define the internal force controller

{f}_{\text{int}}=f^{\prime}_{\text{int}}R_{\scriptscriptstyle C}{\ell^{*}_{\textup{int}}}

(86a)

\displaystyle\ell_{\textup{int}}^{*}=

\displaystyle\text{argmin}_{{\ell}_{\textup{int}}}\left\{\sum_{i\in\mathcal{N}}\ell_{\textup{int},i_{x}}^{2}+\ell_{\textup{int},i_{y}}^{2}+\ell_{\textup{int},i_{z}}^{2}\right\}

(86b)

s. t.

(86c)

\displaystyle G_{\mathcal{R}}R_{\scriptscriptstyle C}\ell_{\textup{int}}={0},

(86d)

\displaystyle\ell_{\textup{int},i_{z}}>0,\ \ \ \ \ \forall i\in\mathcal{N},

(86e)

\displaystyle\Lambda_{i}(\mu_{f}){\ell}_{\textup{int},i}\succ 0,\ \forall i\in\mathcal{N},

(86f)

where
{\ell}_{\textup{int}}\coloneqq[{\ell}_{\textup{int},1}^{\top},\dots,{\ell}_{\textup{int},N}^{\top}]^{\top}
. Note that, since the contact points form a force-closure configuration, (
86
) always has a feasible solution.

Finally, to satisfy (
85
),
{f}_{\text{int}}
must apply sufficient force inside the friction cone to reject perturbations that will arise during the manipulation motion that can push the contact force outside of the friction cone. Rejection of these perturbations is performed by designing the gain
f^{\prime}_{\text{int}}
as follows. For simplicity we define the terms
{k}_{\textup{int}}\coloneqq\Lambda(\mu_{f})R_{\scriptscriptstyle C}^{\top}{G^{\dagger}_{\mathcal{R}}}{f}_{\text{d}},{l}_{\textup{int}}=\Lambda(\mu_{f}){\ell^{*}_{\text{int}}}
,
and we denote by
k_{\textup{int},j}
and
l_{\textup{int},j}
the
j
th scalar element of
{k}_{\textup{int}}
and
{l}_{\textup{int}}
respectively for
j\in\{1,\dots,Nl_{f}\}
.

Noting that
\Lambda(\mu_{f}){\ell^{*}}_{\textup{int}}\succ 0
from (
86
), we define
the
decreasing
function
\kappa_{\textup{int}}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
as

\kappa_{\textup{int}}(x)\coloneqq\begin{cases}-x,\hskip 14.22636pt\text{if }x\leq-1,\\
q_{\textup{int}}(x),\hskip 9.3894pt\text{if }-1\leq x\leq 0,\\
0,\hskip 22.76219pt\text{if }x\geq 0\end{cases},

where
q_{\textup{int}}(x)\geq 0
,
\forall x\in[-1,0]
, is an appropriate polynomial that ensures continuous differentiability of
\kappa_{\textup{int}}
, for instance
q_{\textup{int}}(x)=x^{3}+2x^{2}
. Then one can verify that
\kappa_{\textup{int}}(x)+1\geq-x
,
\forall x\in\mathbb{R}
.
We now design the magnitude scaling for the internal forces as

f^{\prime}_{\text{int}}=\frac{\kappa(\min_{j}\{k_{\textup{int},j}\})+1+\epsilon_{f}}{\min_{j}\{l_{\textup{int},j}\}},

(87)

where
\epsilon_{f}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
is a tuning gain. The intuition behind (
87
) is to upper bound elements of the control and the system dynamics to prevent either from pushing the contact force outside of the friction cone. The term
\kappa(\min_{j}\{k_{j}\})+1
cancels out any effects from
{f}_{\textup{d}}
. The term
\epsilon_{f}
handles the system dynamics, which is guaranteed to be bounded in the following theorem.

Remark 2.10
.

The internal force control presented here accounts for the dynamics of the system by appropriately scaling
f^{\prime}_{\text{int}}
, which rejects perturbations from causing slip. However, as opposed to
[
120
]
, we relax the condition that
\epsilon_{f}
must upper bound all of the dynamics terms by exploiting knowledge of the applied controller via the term
\kappa_{\textup{int}}(\min_{j}\{k_{\textup{int},j}\})
. This reduces the amount of squeezing force applied to prevent crushing the object.

The stability and slip prevention guarantees of the proposed controller are presented in the following theorem.

Theorem 2.5
.

Consider
N
robotic agents in contact with an object, described by the dynamics (
70
), (
73
), and suppose Assumptions
2.10
and
2.11
hold. a the desired object pose
[{p}_{\textup{d}}^{\top},{\eta}^{\top}_{\textup{d}}]^{\top}:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\to\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{S}^{3}
be bounded with bounded first and second derivatives. Moreover, assume that
e_{\varphi}(0)\neq 0
and
{f}^{\scriptscriptstyle C_{i}}_{\scriptscriptstyle C_{i}}(0)\in\textup{Int}(\mathcal{F}_{\scriptscriptstyle C_{i}}(\mu_{f}))
,
\forall i\in\mathcal{N}
.
Then, the control protocol (
80a
)-(
87
) guarantees that
\text{lim}_{t\to\infty}\left({e}_{p_{o}}(t),{e}_{\eta}(t)\right)=\left({0},[\pm 1,{0}^{\top}]^{\top}\right)
,
as well as boundedness of all closed-loop signals. Moreover, by choosing a sufficiently large
\epsilon_{f}
in (
87
), it holds that
{f}^{\scriptscriptstyle C_{i}}_{\scriptscriptstyle C_{i}}(t)\in\mathcal{F}_{\scriptscriptstyle C_{i}},\forall t>0
,
i\in\mathcal{N}
.

Proof.

Consider the stack vector state
{\chi}\coloneqq[{e}_{p_{o}}^{\top},{e}_{\epsilon}^{\top},{e}_{v_{o}}^{\top},{e}_{\vartheta_{\mathcal{R}}}^{\top},{e}_{\vartheta_{\mathcal{R}},o}^{\top}]^{\top}\in\mathcal{X}\coloneqq\mathbb{R}^{12+N\ell_{\mathcal{R}}+\ell_{\mathcal{R}_{o}}}
. Next, note by
(
70
), (
71
), and (
77
) that, when
{f}_{\scriptscriptstyle C_{i}}^{\scriptscriptstyle C_{i}}\in\mathcal{F}_{\scriptscriptstyle C_{i}}
, each
{f}_{\scriptscriptstyle C_{i}}^{\scriptscriptstyle C_{i}}
can be written as a function of the stack state, i.e.,
{f}_{\scriptscriptstyle C_{i}}^{\scriptscriptstyle C_{i}}={f}_{\scriptscriptstyle C_{i}}^{\scriptscriptstyle C_{i}}({\chi})
,
\forall i\in\mathcal{N}
.
Consider also the set

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}\coloneqq\{{\chi}

\displaystyle\in\mathcal{X}:\|{e}_{\epsilon}\|<\bar{e}_{\epsilon},\|{e}_{p_{o}}\|<\bar{e}_{p_{o}},\|{e}_{v_{o}}\|<\bar{e}_{v_{o}},\|{e}_{\vartheta_{\mathcal{R}}}\|<\tilde{e}_{\vartheta_{\mathcal{R}}},

\displaystyle\|{e}_{\vartheta_{\mathcal{R},o}}\|<\tilde{e}_{\vartheta_{\mathcal{R},o}},{f}_{\scriptscriptstyle C_{i}}^{\scriptscriptstyle C_{i}}({\chi})\in{\textup{Int}({\mathcal{F}}_{\scriptscriptstyle C_{i}})},\forall i\in\mathcal{N}\},

for some positive constants
\bar{e}_{\epsilon}
,
\bar{e}_{v_{o}}
,
\bar{e}_{p_{o}}
satisfying
\|e_{\epsilon}(0)\|\leq\bar{e}_{\epsilon}
,
\|{e}_{v_{o}}(0)\|<\bar{e}_{v_{o}}
,
\|{e}_{p_{o}}(0)\|<\bar{e}_{p_{o}}
, and
\tilde{e}_{\vartheta_{\mathcal{R}}},\tilde{e}_{\mathcal{R},o}
larger than
\bar{e}_{\vartheta_{\mathcal{R}}},\bar{e}_{\vartheta_{\mathcal{R},o}}
, respectively, which were introduced in (
84
). Note that
{\chi}(0)\in\mathcal{U}
. Next,
by using (
81
) and (
82
), one obtains the closed-loop dynamics
\dot{{\chi}}={h}_{\chi}({\chi},t)
, where
{h}_{\chi}:\mathcal{X}\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\to\mathcal{X}
is a function that is continuous in
t
and locally Lipschitz in
{\chi}
.
Then, according to Theorem
8.1
of Appendix
8
, there exists a positive time constant
t_{\max}>0
and a unique solution
{\chi}:[0,t_{\max})\to\mathcal{U}
, i.e., defined for
[0,t_{\max})
and satisfying
{\chi}(t)\in\mathcal{U}
,
\forall t\in[0,t_{\max})
. Hence, slip is prevented and the dynamics (
78
) are well-defined, for
t\in[0,t_{\max})
.

Let now the Lyapunov function

\displaystyle V_{f}\coloneqq\frac{1}{2}{e}_{p_{o}}^{\top}{e}_{p_{o}}+\frac{2}{e_{\varphi}^{2}}+\frac{1}{2}{e}_{v_{o}}^{\top}\widetilde{B}{e}_{v_{o}}+\frac{1}{2}{e}_{\vartheta_{\mathcal{R}}}^{\top}\Gamma^{-1}{e}_{\vartheta_{\mathcal{R}}}+\frac{1}{2}{e}_{\vartheta_{\mathcal{R},o}}^{\top}\Gamma_{o}^{-1}{e}_{\vartheta_{\mathcal{R},o}}.

(88)

Since
e_{\varphi}(0)\neq 0
, it holds that
V_{f}(0)\leq\bar{V}_{f0}
for a finite positive
\bar{V}_{f0}
.
Differentiation of
V_{f}
results in:

\displaystyle\dot{V}_{f}=

\displaystyle{e}_{\mathcal{R}}^{\top}({v}_{o}-{v}_{\text{d}})+\frac{1}{2}{e}_{v_{o}}^{\top}\dot{\widetilde{B}}{e}_{v_{o}}+{e}_{v_{o}}^{\top}(-\widetilde{C}_{q}{v}_{o}-\widetilde{g}_{q}-\widetilde{B}\dot{v}_{f_{o}}+G_{\mathcal{R}}J_{h}^{-T}{\tau})

\displaystyle+{e}_{\vartheta_{\mathcal{R}}}^{\top}\Gamma^{-1}\dot{\vartheta}_{\mathcal{R}}+{e}_{\vartheta_{\mathcal{R},o}}^{\top}\Gamma_{o}^{-1}\dot{\vartheta}_{\mathcal{R}_{o}}.

Exploitation of the skew symmetry of
\dot{\widetilde{B}}-2\widetilde{C}_{q}
, use of
{v}_{o}={e}_{v_{o}}+{v}_{f_{o}}
, use of (
79
), and substitution of the control law (
81
) results in:

\displaystyle\dot{V}_{f}=

\displaystyle-{e_{\mathcal{R}}}^{\top}K{e}_{\mathcal{R}}-{e}^{\top}_{v_{o}}K_{v}{e}_{v_{o}}+{e}_{v_{o}}^{\top}({Y_{o_{r}}}{e}_{\vartheta_{\mathcal{R},o}}{+G_{\mathcal{R}}J_{h}^{-T}Y_{r}}{e}_{\vartheta_{\mathcal{R}}})

\displaystyle+{e}_{\vartheta_{\mathcal{R}}}^{\top}\Gamma^{-1}\dot{\vartheta}_{\mathcal{R}}+{e}_{\vartheta_{\mathcal{R},o}}^{\top}\Gamma_{o}^{-1}\dot{\vartheta}_{\mathcal{R}_{o}},

where we used the fact that
G_{\mathcal{R}}{f}_{\text{int}}={0}
through (
86
). Finally, by substituting the adaptation laws (
82
), we obtain

\displaystyle\dot{V}_{f}=

\displaystyle-{e}_{\mathcal{R}}^{\top}K{e}_{\mathcal{R}}-{e}^{\top}_{v_{o}}K_{v}{e}_{v_{o}}+{e}_{\vartheta_{\mathcal{R}}}^{\top}\bigg(\Gamma^{-1}\text{Proj}({\vartheta_{\mathcal{R}}},{-Y_{r}}^{\top}J_{h}^{-1}G_{\mathcal{R}}^{\top}{e}_{v_{o}})

\displaystyle+\Gamma Y_{r}^{\top}J_{h}^{-1}G_{\mathcal{R}}^{\top}{e}_{v_{o}}\bigg)+{e}_{\vartheta_{\mathcal{R}_{o}}}^{\top}\bigg(\Gamma_{o}^{-1}\text{Proj}({\vartheta}_{\mathcal{R}_{o}},{-Y_{o_{r}}}^{\top}{e}_{v_{o}}){+}\Gamma_{o}Y_{o_{r}}^{\top}{e}_{v_{o}}\bigg)

which, by invoking the projection operator property (
83
) becomes
\dot{V}_{f}\leq-{e}_{\mathcal{R}}^{\top}K{e}_{\mathcal{R}}-{e}_{v_{o}}^{\top}K_{v}{e}_{v_{o}}
.
Thus
\dot{V}_{f}
is negative semi-definite, and
V_{f}
is bounded in a compact set as
V_{f}(t)\leq V_{f}(0)
,
\forall t\in[0,t_{\max})
. In addition,
e_{\varphi}(t)\neq 0
,
\forall t\in[0,t_{\max})
. Hence, the terms
{e}_{p_{o}}(t)
,
{e}_{\epsilon}(t)
,
e_{\varphi}(t)
are bounded in a compact set defined by
V_{f}(0)
and not dependent on
t_{\max}
,
\forall t\in[0,t_{\max})
. Therefore, since
{p}_{\text{d}}(t)
and
{\eta}_{\text{d}}(t)
are bounded and have bounded derivatives, one concludes that
{p}_{o}(t)
,
{\eta}_{o}(t)

{v}_{o}(t)
, and
{v}_{f_{o}}(t)
,
\dot{v}_{f_{o}}(t)
are also bounded in compact sets,
\forall t\in[0,t_{\max})
. This also implies boundedness of
\check{x},\dot{\check{x}}
, as introduced in (
78
), which,
along with Assumption
2.11
and properties of Euler-Lagrange systems
[
121
]
,
implies that
Y_{\mathcal{R}}()
,
Y_{r}
,
Y_{\mathcal{R}_{o}}()
,
Y_{o_{r}}
are also bounded in compact sets that are independent of
t_{\max}
,
\forall t\in[0,t_{\max})
.
We prove next the slip prevention using the design of the internal force component
{f}_{\text{int}}
. By using (
70
), (
73
) and (
77
), one obtains the following expression for the interaction forces:

\displaystyle{f}_{\scriptscriptstyle C}=

\displaystyle W_{h}^{-1}\bigg(J_{h}B^{-1}\left[\tau-{g}_{q}-\left(C_{q}J_{h}^{-1}G_{\mathcal{R}}^{\top}+B\frac{d}{dt}(J_{h}^{-1}G_{\mathcal{R}}^{\top})\right){v}_{o}\right]

\displaystyle+G_{\mathcal{R}}^{\top}M_{o}^{-1}(C_{o}{v}_{o}+{g}_{o})\bigg),

(89)

where
W_{h}\coloneqq J_{h}B^{-1}J_{h}^{\top}+G_{\mathcal{R}}^{\top}M_{o}^{-1}G_{\mathcal{R}}
, which, by replacing
\tau
, using
{v}_{f_{o}}={e}_{v_{o}}+{v}_{o}
and (
79
), adding and subtracting
W_{h}^{-1}G_{\mathcal{R}}^{\top}M_{o}^{-1}{G_{\mathcal{R}}G_{\mathcal{R}}^{\dagger}}{f}_{\textup{d}}
and adding
W_{h}^{-1}G_{\mathcal{R}}^{\top}M_{o}^{-1}G_{\mathcal{R}}{f}_{\textup{int}}={0}
, becomes

{f}_{\scriptscriptstyle C}=G_{\mathcal{R}}^{\dagger}{f}_{\textup{d}}+{f}_{\text{int}}+{h_{f}}

(90)

where

\displaystyle h_{f}\coloneqq

\displaystyle W_{h}^{-1}J_{h}B^{-1}(g_{q}-Y_{\mathcal{R}}(\check{{x}},\dot{\check{{x}}},{e}_{v_{o}},\dot{e}_{v_{o}})\vartheta_{\mathcal{R}}+Y_{r}{e}_{\vartheta_{\mathcal{R}}})+W_{h}^{-1}G_{\mathcal{R}}^{\top}M_{o}({e}_{\mathcal{R}}

\displaystyle+K_{v}{e}_{v_{o}}+Y_{\mathcal{R}_{o}}(\eta_{o},{\omega}_{o},{e}_{v_{o}},\dot{e}_{v_{o}})\vartheta_{\mathcal{R}_{o}}-Y_{o_{r}}{e}_{\vartheta_{\mathcal{R},o}}-g_{o}).

By combining the aforementioned expression with (
85
), one obtains the following condition for slip prevention:

\displaystyle\Lambda(\mu_{f})R_{\scriptscriptstyle C}^{\top}{f}_{\text{int}}\succeq{-\Lambda(\mu_{f})R_{\scriptscriptstyle C}^{\top}G^{\dagger}{f}_{\text{d}}-\Lambda(\mu_{f})R_{\scriptscriptstyle C}^{\top}{h_{f}}.}

(91)

Note that due to the aforementioned Lyapunov analysis, as well as the adaptation laws (
82
) through the projection operator,
{e}_{\mathcal{R}}(t)
,
{e}_{v_{o}}(t)
,
\dot{{e}}_{v_{o}}(t)
,
{e}_{\vartheta_{\mathcal{R}}}(t)
,
{e}_{\vartheta_{\mathcal{R},o}}(t)
are bounded in compact set independent of
t_{\max}
,
\forall t\in[0,t_{\max})
. By combining this with the aforementioned analysis, we conclude
that
{h}_{f}
is bounded for all
\forall t\in[0,t_{\max})
in a compact set, independent of
t_{\max}
.
Hence, by denoting
\varepsilon_{h}
the maximum bound of the elements of
\pm\Lambda(\mu_{f})R_{\scriptscriptstyle C}^{\top}{h}_{f}
and using the designed internal force component
{f}_{\text{int}}=f^{\prime}_{\text{int}}R_{\scriptscriptstyle C}{\ell_{\textup{int}}}
, a sufficient condition for (
91
) to hold is for the
j
th element to satisfy

\displaystyle l_{\textup{int},j}f^{\prime}_{\text{int}}\geq{-k_{\textup{int},j}}+\varepsilon_{h},

\forall j\in\{1,\dots,Nl_{f}\}
. By substituting (
87
), the left side satisfies

\displaystyle l_{\textup{int},j}\frac{\kappa(\min_{j}\{k_{\textup{int},j}\})+1+\epsilon_{f}+\delta_{f}}{\min_{j}\{l_{\textup{int},j}\}}

\displaystyle\geq\kappa(\min_{j}\{k_{\textup{int},j}\})+1+\epsilon_{f}+\delta_{f}

\displaystyle\geq{-k_{\textup{int},j}}+\epsilon_{f},

where we use
\kappa_{\textup{int}}(x)\geq 0
,
\kappa_{\textup{int}}(x)+1\geq-x
,
\forall x\in\mathbb{R}
, and
\kappa_{\textup{int}}(\min_{j}(k_{\textup{int},j}))\geq\kappa_{\textup{int}}(k_{\textup{int},j})
,
\forall j\in\{1,\dots,Nl_{f}\}
, since
\kappa_{\textup{int}}()
is decreasing. Hence, by choosing a large enough
\epsilon_{f}
we guarantee
\epsilon_{f}\geq\varepsilon_{h}
and hence contact slip is avoided
\forall t\in[0,t_{\max})
. In fact, the internal forces analysis above and the fact that
\Lambda(\mu_{f})
defines pyramid constraints imply that
{f}^{\scriptscriptstyle{C}_{i}}_{\scriptscriptstyle C_{i}}\in\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{\scriptscriptstyle C_{i}}
, where
\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{\scriptscriptstyle C_{i}}
is a compact subset of
\text{Int}(\mathcal{F}_{\scriptscriptstyle C_{i}})
,
\forall i\in\mathcal{N}
. Therefore, since
{e}_{\vartheta_{\mathcal{R}}}
and
{e}_{\vartheta_{\mathcal{R}},o}
are uniformly bounded through the projection operator by
\bar{e}_{\vartheta_{\mathcal{R}}}
and
\bar{e}_{\vartheta_{\mathcal{R}},o}
, respectively, by choosing large enough
\bar{e}_{p_{o}}
,
\bar{e}_{\epsilon}
, and
\bar{e}_{v_{o}}
in the definition of
\mathcal{U}
,
\chi(t)
belongs to a compact subset
\bar{\mathcal{U}}
of
\mathcal{U}
,
\forall t\in[0,t_{\max})
. Thus by invoking Theorem
8.2
of Appendix
8
, it follows that
t_{\max}=\infty
.

Note, finally, that
{\tau}(\chi_{\mathcal{R}}(t),t)
, as designed in (
81
), is bounded,
\forall t\geq 0
. Therefore, one can conclude that

\dot{e}_{v_{o}}(t)
and thus
\ddot{q}(t)
is bounded,
\forall t\geq 0
.
Hence, it follows that
\ddot{V}_{f}(t)
is also bounded,
\forall t\geq 0
.
Thus by invoking
Barbalat’s lemma (Lemma
8.1
of Appendix
8
), it follows that
\lim_{t\to\infty}\dot{V}_{f}(t)=0
and so
\lim_{t\to\infty}{e}_{\mathcal{R}}(t)\to{0}
and
\lim_{t\to\infty}{e}_{v_{o}}(t)\to 0
. This implies that
\lim_{t\to\infty}{e}_{\epsilon}(t)\to{0}
, which, given that
{e}_{\eta}
is a unit quaternion and
e_{\varphi}(t)\neq 0
,
\forall t\geq 0
, ensures asymptotic stability of the pose error as
\lim_{t\to\infty}({e}_{p_{o}}(t),{e}_{\zeta}(t))=({0},[\text{sgn}(e_{\varphi}(0)),{0}^{\top}]^{\top})
.

∎

Remark 2.11
.

Note that the bound
\varepsilon_{h}
of
{h}_{f}
in (
90
) can be computed a priori. In practice, the terms
\vartheta_{\mathcal{R}}
,
\vartheta_{\mathcal{R}_{o}}
, which concern masses and moments of inertia of the object and the agents, can be known a priori up to a certain accuracy, leading thus to respective bounds.
Hence, one can compute upper bounds for
V_{f}(0)
and hence for
{e}_{\mathcal{R}}
,
{e}_{v_{o}}
,
{e}_{\vartheta_{\mathcal{R}}}
, and
{e}_{\vartheta_{\mathcal{R}},o}
. Since the structure of the dynamic terms is known, this can also lead to a bound of the terms
W_{h}^{-1}
,
B^{-1}
,
M_{o}
,
Y_{\mathcal{R}}(\cdot)
,
Y_{\mathcal{R}_{o}}(\cdot)
,
Y_{r}
, and
Y_{o_{r}}
that appear in
{h}_{f}
. Hence, tuning of
\epsilon_{f}
to overcome
\varepsilon_{h}
can be performed off-line.

4.2.1
Simulation Results

The proposed control algorithm ensures asymptotic stability for cooperative manipulation with rolling contacts, as well as no slip, while being robust to dynamic uncertainties of the object-robot system. In this section, we implement the proposed control scheme on three 6 DoF mobile manipulators consisting of a 3 DoF,
3
kg base (X-Y translation, rotation about Z) and a 3-DoF manipulator with
3
identical links of length
0.3
m and mass of
0.5
kg each, as depicted in Fig.
25
. The objective is to transport a
2
kg box along the desired reference trajectory defined by
{p}_{\text{d}}(t)\coloneqq[0.1\sin(0.125t),0.1\sin(0.125t),0.1\sin(0.125t)]^{\top}
m,
{\eta}_{\text{d}}(t)\coloneqq[\cos(0.1\sin(.125t)),0,0,\sin(0.1\sin(0.125t))]^{\top}
. The control gains used are:
k_{p}=1
,
k_{\eta}=.5
,
K_{v}=\text{diag}[5,5,5,2,2,2]
,
\epsilon_{f}=0.1
,
\Gamma_{o}=0.5I_{\ell_{\mathcal{R}_{o}}}
,
\Gamma=0.5I_{N\ell_{\mathcal{R}}}
. The control is implemented with 30% error in all uncertain parameter (including the object center of mass), and the projection operator enforces the following bounds on the uncertain terms:
\bar{\hat{\theta}}_{\mathcal{R}}=2.25
,
\bar{\hat{\theta}}_{\mathcal{R}_{o}}=1.5
.

The simulation results are depicted in Figs.
26
-
30
for
50
seconds. More specifically,
Figs.
26
and
27
show the resulting error trajectories of the object-agent system, which satisfy
\lim_{t\to\infty}{e}_{p_{o}}(t)={0}
,
\lim_{t\to\infty}{e}_{\epsilon}(t)={0}
, and
\lim_{t\to\infty}{e}_{\varphi}(t)=\text{sgn}({e}_{\varphi}(0))=1
in the presence of rolling effects. Fig.
28
illustrates the boundedness of the uncertain parameters,
{\hat{\vartheta}}_{\mathcal{R}},\hat{\vartheta}_{\mathcal{R}_{o}}
that is enforced by the proposed control scheme. Fig.
29
shows the required friction,
\mu_{ri}:=\frac{\sqrt{f_{\scriptscriptstyle C,x_{i}}^{2}+f_{\scriptscriptstyle C,y_{i}}^{2}}}{f_{\scriptscriptstyle C,n_{i}}}
, which denotes the minimum friction coefficient necessary to prevent slip throughout the motion
[
120
]
. If the required friction surpasses the true coefficient, then the contact point will slip and the grasp is compromised. As shown in Fig.
29
, however, the required friction for each contact is below the true coefficient of
\mu_{f}=0.9
, which indicates that slip is prevented as guaranteed by the proposed method. Finally, Fig.
30
depicts the control inputs of the agents. As predicted by the theoretical analysis, asymptotic error stability as well as contact slip prevention are achieved.

Figure 25
:
Initial configuration of the system that consists of three mobile manipulators and a rigid object.

Figure 26
:
The evolution of the position error,
{e}_{p_{o}}(t)
,
\forall t\in[0,50]
.

Figure 27
:
The evolution of
e_{\varphi}(t)
,
{e}_{\epsilon}(t)
,
\forall t\in[0,50]
.

Figure 28
:
The evolution of
\hat{\vartheta}_{\mathcal{R}_{o}}(t)
,
\hat{\vartheta}_{\mathcal{R}}(t)
,
\forall t\in[0,50]
.
